Taut functors and the difference operator
Robert Paré

Abstract
We establish a calculus of differences for taut endofunctors of the category of sets, analogous to the classical calculus of finite differences for real valued functions. We study how the difference operator interacts with limits and colimits as categorical versions, of the usual product and sum rules. The first main result is a lax chain rule which has no counterpart for mere functions. We also show that many important classes of functors (polynomials, analytic functors, reduced powers, …) are taut, and calculate explicit formulas for their differences. Covariant Dirichlet series are introduced and studied. The second main result is a Newton summation formula expressed as an adjoint to the difference operator.

Introduction

The category of endofunctors of
{\bf Set}
, the category of
sets, contains many interesting subcategories. Consider, for example,
the recent work on polynomial functors, spearheaded by Spivak
(see
[
14
]
and the references there), building on
previous work of Kock chronicled in his arXiv paper
[
10
]

(see also
[
8
]
which deals with polynomial monads).
Before that there were the analytic functors of
Joyal
[
9
]
stemming from his categorical treatment of
combinatorics, and developed extensively since then
(see
[
7
]
and its extensive bibliography).
But the study of endofunctors of
{\bf Set}

goes back to the early days of category theory.
Questions of rank for monads arose, showing the necessity of
looking more deeply into the structure of these endofunctors.

This suggests that the study of
{\bf Set}
endofunctors
goes to the very foundation of set theory. For example, a
non-principal ultrafilter, whose existence is well-known to
require some form of the axiom of choice, produces an
interesting endofunctor, the ultra-power functor. It is
left exact and preserves finite coproducts, and so it is
isomorphic to the identity on finite sets but not infinite ones.
In
[
19
]
Trnková, and independently Blass
[
4
,
5
]

showed that the existence of a
non-trivial
exact
endofunctor of
{\bf Set}
is
equivalent to the existence of a measurable cardinal. Also
see
[
16
]
in this connection.

These considerations, among others, indicate that a
systematic study of the structure of endofunctors of

{\bf Set}
might be desirable. And indeed, such a
study was initiated in
[
18
,
19
]
by
Trnková, where she made an exhaustive study of
their preservation properties.

The present work revisits this project from a different
perspective taking into account the many developments
of the last century. We develop a difference calculus
for a rather large class of functors which parallels the
classical finite difference calculus for real valued functions.
The class of functors we consider are the taut functors
introduced by Manes
[
12
]
, who was motivated
by theoretical computer science considerations. (Taut
functors were also considered in
[
18
]
under
the name “preimage preserving functors”.)

Section
1
begins by recalling Manes’ definitions
of taut functor and taut natural transformation and
recording some of their basic properties to be referred to later.
Then follows a detailed study of the stability of tautness
under limits and colimits. The main result here is a
characterization of those colimits that preserve tautness,
Theorem
1.12
, which to our knowledge, has
never appeared in print.

In Section
2
we consider some known
classes of endofunctors: polynomials, divided power
series, analytic functors, reduced powers, all of which
are taut. We also consider taut monads. We introduce
covariant Dirichlet functors, and what we call sequential
Dirichlet functors, which are taut too.

It is in Section
3
that we introduce the
difference operator. We study its functorial properties
and then how it interacts with limits and colimits. As a
special case we get a product (Leibniz) rule. Half of the
section is taken up by the proof of one of the main results of
the paper, the lax chain rule and its properties.

In Section
4
we return to the special classes
considered in Section
2
and obtain
explicit formulas for the differences.

We round out the paper with a version of the Newton
summation formula, a discrete version of Taylor series.
This is meant to recover nice taut functors, analytic say,
from their iterated differences at
0
. It is given by
a left adjoint to the difference functor whose unit is
an isomorphism for analytic functors.

Except for a few comments in passing, we mostly
ignore questions of size. The “category” of endofunctors
is an illegitimate one. Its homs may be proper classes.
None of our results depends on this, but the purist will
have no difficulty legitimizing things using standard
techniques – Grothendieck universes or Gödel-Bernays
set theory (with classes), for example.

We would like to thank Andreas Blass, Aaron Fairbanks,
Theo Johnson-Freyd, Deni Salja and Peter Selinger for
helpful comments.

1
Taut functors

The notions of taut functor and natural transformation were
introduced by Manes
[
12
]
. These are precisely what
we need to develop our difference calculus of functors.

Everything that follows centres around pullbacks in which
one leg is a monomorphism. Following Manes, we call them

inverse image diagrams
.

1.1
Definitions and functorial properties

Definition 1.0

(Manes
[
12
]
)
.

A functor is taut if it preserves inverse image diagrams.
A natural transformation is taut if the naturality squares
corresponding to monomorphisms are pullbacks.

We record some of the general properties of tautness.

Proposition 1.0
.

(1)

Taut functors preserve monos.

(2)

The composite of taut functors is taut.

(3)

Corollary 1.0
.

Categories with inverse images, taut functors and taut
natural transformations give a sub-
2
-category

{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}
of
{\cal C}{\it at}
, the
2
-category of
categories.

1.2
Limits of taut functors

Limits of taut functors are again taut. This is just
a case of limits commuting with limits but some attention
must be paid as to where the diagrams and limits
are taken. If
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
are categories
with inverse images, we have the
{\cal C}{\it at}
functor
category
{\cal C}{\it at}({\bf A},{\bf B})
of all functors from

{\bf A}
to
{\bf B}
and all natural transformations,
and the subcategory
{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}({\bf A},{\bf B})

of all taut functors and taut natural transformations. And,
we also have the full image of
{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}({\bf A},{\bf B})
in
{\cal C}{\it at}({\bf A},{\bf B})
,
{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}_{full}({\bf A},{\bf B})
, of taut functors and all natural
transformations.

Proposition 1.0
.

Assume that
{\bf B}
has
{\bf I}
-limits. Then
{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}_{full}({\bf A},{\bf B})
is closed under
{\bf I}
-limits in

{\cal C}{\it at}({\bf A},{\bf B})
. If

Corollary 1.0
.

If
{\bf B}
has pullbacks, then the pullback of a
taut transformation along any natural transformation
is again taut.

Proof.

Consider the pullback of taut functors

\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{G}
\scriptstyle{u}

\textstyle{H}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{H\times_{G}F}
\textstyle{F}

\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{G}
\scriptstyle{t}
Pb

with
t

\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{G}

\scriptstyle{\id}

\scriptstyle{t}

\scriptstyle{\id}

We emphasize that we are not assuming that the
transformations
\Gamma(i)\colon\Gamma(I)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\Gamma(J)
are taut, but even if we did we still would not
get limits in
{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}({\bf A},{\bf B})
. The projections are not taut.
For example, if
{\bf B}
has finite products, then the
product of two taut functors is taut but the projection

F\times G\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>F

is not. For a mono
m\colon A_{0}\ \>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>A
, the
naturality square

\textstyle{F(A_{0})\ }
\textstyle{F(A)}
\scriptstyle{Fm}
\textstyle{F(A_{0})\times G(A_{0})\ }
\textstyle{F(A_{0})\ }
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{F(A_{0})\times G(A_{0})\ }
\textstyle{F(A)\times G(A)}
\scriptstyle{Fm\times Gm}
\textstyle{F(A)\times G(A)}
\textstyle{F(A)}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}

is not usually a pullback; the pullback is
F(A_{0})\times G(A)
.

A simpler example is that the unique map

F\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>1
is not taut. However this is the only obstruction
as we see below (
1.10
).

Remark 1.0
.

We assumed that
{\bf B}
had all
{\bf I}
-limits but
the proposition holds for any limits that exist in

{\cal C}{\it at}({\bf A},{\bf B})
as long as they are pointwise,
i.e. calculated in
{\bf B}
.

Remark 1.0
.

We can’t help pointing out that what we are dealing
with are double limits, taken in the double category

{\mathbb{T}}{\rm aut}({\bf A},{\bf B})
whose objects are taut functors,
horizontal arrows arbitrary natural transformations,
vertical arrows taut transformations, and commutative
squares as cells. This may be worth pursuing but here
is not the place.

.

Remark 1.0
.

Note that
\Delta
is taut but not cartesian. For example,
the pullback of
Ff\times Ff
along
\Delta
is the
kernel pair
K
of
Ff

\textstyle{FA_{0}\times FA_{0}}
\textstyle{FA\times FA}

\textstyle{K}
\textstyle{FA_{0}\times FA_{0}}
\textstyle{K}
\textstyle{FA}
\textstyle{FA}
\textstyle{FA\times FA}

which will be

only if

is monic.

So every cone in
{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}({\bf A},{\bf B})
gives a unique
taut transformation into the limit but this still doesn’t
make it into a limit in
{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}({\bf A},{\bf B})
, because,
as we said, the projections are
not necessarily taut. But for non-empty connected limits,
pullbacks or equalizers for example, it does.

\textstyle{\Phi(J)(A_{0})}
\textstyle{\Phi(J)(A)}
\textstyle{B}

\scriptstyle{\Phi(K)(m)}

\scriptstyle{\Phi(J)(m)}

\scriptstyle{\Phi(j)(A_{0})}

\scriptstyle{\Phi(j)(A)}

\scriptstyle{b^{\prime}_{J}}

\scriptstyle{b_{J}}

commutes and the composite on the right is

b_{K}
, so
b^{\prime}_{K}=\Phi(j)(A_{0})\cdot b^{\prime}_{J}
.
Conversely, as
\Phi(j)
is taut, the above
square is a pullback, so if we have a
b^{\prime}_{K}
,
then there exists a unique
b^{\prime}_{J}
. This means
that if
j\colon J\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>K
and
K\in{\bf J}

then so is
J
. We’re given that
I_{0}\in{\bf J}

and as anything connected to it will also be in

{\bf J}
, we have
{\bf J}={\bf I}
.
∎

1.3
Colimits of taut functors

We will only be concerned with
{\bf Set}
valued
taut functors, and for these, there are special colimit
commutations. The main concept is the following.

Definition 1.0
.

A category
{\bf I}
is confluent if for every
pair of arrows with the same domain,
\alpha_{i}\colon I\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>I_{i}\ (i=1,2)
there exist
\beta_{i}\colon I_{i}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>J

making
\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}=\beta_{2}\alpha_{2}

Lemma 1.0
.

Let
{\bf I}
be confluent and
\Gamma_{0}
a subdiagram
of
\Gamma
,

\Gamma_{0}\subseteq\Gamma\colon{\bf I}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf Set}
.
Then limit
\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}
is isomorphic to
the set of elements
[I,x]
in
\varinjlim\Gamma

such that there exists
\beta\colon I\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>J

\Gamma(\beta)(x)\in\Gamma_{0}J

Proof.

An element of
\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}
is an
equivalence class of pairs
(I,x)
with
x\in\Gamma_{0}I
,
which for now we denote
[I,x]_{0}
. To it there is a
well-defined element
[I,x]
of
\varinjlim\Gamma
,
whether
{\bf I}
is confluent or not, but if
{\bf I}
is
confluent the equivalence relations as described in Lemma
1.13

are the same for
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{0}
(crucial point!), so
the function
[I,x]_{0}\mapsto[I,x]

is one-to-one. The classes are not the same –
[I,x]

may contain more elements – but we can identify

\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}
with those elements of

\varinjlim\Gamma
that have a representative
in
\Gamma_{0}
. So
[I,x]
is in the image of

\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}
iff there exist

x^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{0}I^{\prime}
,
\beta\colon I\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>J
,

\beta^{\prime}\colon I^{\prime}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>J
with

\Gamma(\beta)(x)=\Gamma(\beta^{\prime})(x^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{0}J
.
Then, if
x
is in the image of
\Gamma_{0}
, there does exist
a
\beta
with
\Gamma(\beta)(x)\in\Gamma_{0}J
,
and conversely, if there is such a
\beta
we can always
take
\beta^{\prime}=1_{J}
.
∎

From now on, we will identify
\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}

with its image in
\varinjlim\Gamma
.

of theorem.

First of all, let
{\bf I}
be confluent and

\textstyle{\Gamma_{0}}
\textstyle{\Gamma}
\textstyle{\Phi_{0}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{0}}
\scriptstyle{t_{0}}
\textstyle{\Phi_{0}}
\textstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{t}

be an inverse image diagram in
{\bf Set}^{\bf I}
,
i.e.
\Phi_{0}(I)=t(I)^{-1}\Gamma_{0}(I)
, and take
the colimits

\textstyle{\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Gamma\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Phi_{0}}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\varinjlim t_{0}}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Phi_{0}}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Phi}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Phi}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Gamma\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\varinjlim t}

So
\varinjlim\Phi_{0}
is contained in the inverse image of

\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}
. An element in that inverse image is

[x,I]\in\varinjlim\Phi
such that
[t(I)(x),I]
is in

\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}
, i.e. there is
\beta\colon I\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>J

such that
\Gamma(\beta)t(I)(x)\in\Gamma_{0}J
.
But
\Gamma(\beta)t(I)(x)=t(J)\Phi(\beta)(x)

and as it is in
\Gamma_{0}J
,
\Phi(\beta)(x)\in\Phi_{0}J
, which implies
[x,I]\in\varinjlim\Phi_{0}
. Thus
the inverse image of
\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}
is equal to

\varinjlim\Phi_{0}
, and confluent colimits commute with
inverse images in
{\bf Set}
.

Let
{\bf I}
be any small category and

\textstyle{I}
\textstyle{I_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{2}}
\textstyle{I_{1}}
\textstyle{I}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}
\textstyle{I_{1}}
\textstyle{I_{2}}

be arrows in
{\bf I}
. Consider the following inverse
image diagram

\textstyle{{\bf I}(I_{2},-)}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{0}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{0}}
\textstyle{{\bf I}(I,-)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\textstyle{\Phi_{0}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{0}}
\textstyle{\Phi_{0}}
\textstyle{{\bf I}(I_{1},-)}
\textstyle{{\bf I}(I_{1},-)}
\textstyle{{\bf I}(I,-)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{{\bf I}(\alpha_{1},-)}

Here
\Gamma_{0}
is the image of the natural
transformation
{\bf I}(\alpha_{2},-)
and
\Phi_{0}

the inverse image of
\Gamma_{0}
under

{\bf I}(\alpha_{1},-)
. The colimit of a representable
is always
1
, so taking colimits we get

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}}
\textstyle{1\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Phi_{0}}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}}
\textstyle{\varinjlim\Phi_{0}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}

Then
\varinjlim\Gamma_{0}
must be
1
also, and
if
{\bf I}
-colimits commute with inverse images,

\varinjlim\Phi_{0}
also has to be
1
. Now,
\Phi_{0}(J)

is the set of all morphisms
\beta_{1}\colon I_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>J
such
that there exists
\beta_{2}\colon I_{2}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>J
with

\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}=\beta_{2}\alpha_{2}
,

\textstyle{I}
\textstyle{I_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{2}}
\textstyle{I_{1}}
\textstyle{I}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}
\textstyle{I_{1}}
\textstyle{I_{2}}
\textstyle{J}
\textstyle{I_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{2}}
\textstyle{I_{1}}
\textstyle{J}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{1}}
\textstyle{I_{1}}
\textstyle{I_{2}}
.

So if
\varinjlim\Phi_{0}
is to be
1
there has to exist at
least one such pair
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
. I.e.
{\bf I}

is confluent.
∎

Remark 1.0
.

Saying that
{\bf I}
-colimits commute with inverse images
means exactly that the colimit functor

\varinjlim\colon{\bf Set}^{\bf I}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf Set}

is taut.

Proposition 1.0
.

{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}_{full}({\bf A},{\bf Set})
is closed under confluent
colimits. If
t
is a natural transformation of confluent diagrams in

{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}_{full}({\bf A},{\bf B})
,
and all the values of
t
are taut, then the induced
morphism
\varinjlim t
is also taut.

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
1.4
carries over
verbatim, only using that confluent colimits commute with
inverse images in
{\bf Set}
.
∎

Proposition
1.8
also “dualizes” to
confluent colimits with some modifications.

Proposition 1.0
.

Let
{\bf I}
be confluent and
\Phi\colon{\bf I}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}_{full}({\bf A},{\bf Set})
a diagram of taut

a taut

\gamma\colon\Phi\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>F

is also taut.

we get that

is taut, and
\varinjlim_{I}F=\pi_{0}{\bf I}\times F
, the

\pi_{0}{\bf I}
copies of

. So it
will be sufficient to show that the codiagonal

\nabla\colon\pi_{0}{\bf I}\times F\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>F

is taut, which is now clear from the pullback diagram

\textstyle{FA_{0}}
\textstyle{FA\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}{\bf I}\times F(A_{0})}
\textstyle{FA_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}{\bf I}\times F(A_{0})}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}{\bf I}\times F(A)}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}{\bf I}\times F(A)}
\textstyle{FA\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}

∎

Proposition 1.0
.

{\cal T\!\!}{\it aut}({\bf A},{\bf Set})
is closed under confluent
colimits in
{\cal C}{\it at}({\bf A},{\bf Set})
.

Proof.

In view of the previous proposition, we only have to show
that the colimit injections

j=jI_{0}\colon\Phi(I_{0})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\varinjlim_{I}\Phi I

are taut. Let
A_{0}\ \ \>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\ A
be a mono and
consider the commutative square, which we want to
show is a pullback

\textstyle{\varinjlim_{I}\Phi(I)(A_{0})}
\textstyle{\varinjlim_{I}\Phi(I)(A)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\textstyle{\Phi(I_{0})(A_{0})}
\textstyle{\varinjlim_{I}\Phi(I)(A_{0})}
\scriptstyle{jA_{0}}
\textstyle{\Phi(I_{0})(A_{0})}
\textstyle{\Phi(I_{0})(A)}
\textstyle{\Phi(I_{0})(A)}
\textstyle{\varinjlim_{I}\Phi(I)(A)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{jA}

The elements of
\varinjlim_{I}\Phi(I)(A)
are equivalence
classes
[I,x\in\Phi(I)(A)]
and from
Lemma
1.14
we can identify

\varinjlim_{I}\Phi(I)(A_{0})
with those classes for which
there exist
J
and
\beta\colon I\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>J
such that

\Phi(\beta)(A)(x)\in\Phi(J)(A_{0})
. Let

x_{0}\in\Phi(I_{0})(A)
be such that

j(A)(x_{0})\in\varinjlim_{I}\Phi(I)(A_{0})
.
j(A)(x)=[I_{0},x_{0}]
so there exists
\beta\colon I_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>J

with
\Phi(\beta)(A)(x_{0})\in\Phi(J)(A_{0})
.

\Phi(\beta)
is taut so the following is a pullback

\textstyle{\Phi(J)(A_{0})}
\textstyle{\Phi(J)(A)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\textstyle{\Phi(I_{0})(A_{0})}
\textstyle{\Phi(J)(A_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\Phi(\beta)(A_{0})}
\textstyle{\Phi(I_{0})(A_{0})}
\textstyle{\Phi(I_{0})(A)}
\textstyle{\Phi(I_{0})(A)}
\textstyle{\Phi(J)(A)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi(\beta)(A)}

So
\Phi(\beta)(A)(x_{0})\in\Phi(J)(A_{0})
implies

x_{0}\in\Phi(I_{0})(A_{0})
, which shows that our original
square is a pullback, completing the proof.
∎

Corollary 1.0
.

Filtered colimits of taut functors into
{\bf Set}
are taut.

Corollary 1.0
.

The quotient of a taut functor into
{\bf Set}
by a group
action is taut.

Coproducts of set-valued functors will play a central role
in what follows so we end the section on colimits with
some results specifically about them.

Proposition 1.0
.

A coproduct of functors into
{\bf Set}
is taut
if and only if each summand is taut.

Proof.

A discrete category is confluent so a coproduct of
taut functors is taut, by Proposition
1.18

e.g. This is also easy to see directly.

Conversely, if
\sum_{i\in I}F_{i}
is taut, then so is
each
F_{i}
as it is a pullback of taut functors

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\sum_{i\in I}1\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\ j_{i}}
\textstyle{F_{i}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{F_{i}}
\textstyle{\sum_{i\in I}F_{i}}
\textstyle{\sum_{i\in I}F_{i}}
\textstyle{\sum_{i\in I}1\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
Pb

∎

It is well-known that for a small category
{\bf A}
,
every functor
F\colon{\bf A}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf Set}
is a
coproduct of indecomposable functors indexed by a
set
\pi_{0}F
called the connected components of

F
(see e.g.
[
2
]
).
\pi_{0}
is left adjoint
to the diagonal functor
D\colon{\bf Set}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf Set}^{\bf A}
,
i.e. it is the colimit functor.
F
is
connected
if

\pi_{0}F=1
. Here we are concerned with the case
where
{\bf A}={\bf Set}
, which is, of course, not small.
If
{\bf A}
is a large category, then

\pi_{0}F
may well be a proper class, and things
fall apart. We don’t get the adjointness to
D

for example. But, if
{\bf A}
has a terminal object,
everything is much nicer.

Proposition 1.0
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a category with terminal object
1
.
Then for a functor
F\colon{\bf A}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf Set}
,

(1)

\pi_{0}F\cong F1

(2)

F
is connected (indecomposable)
if and only if
F1=1

(3)

Every
F
is a coproduct of connected
functors.

Proof.

The unique morphism
\tau X\colon X\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>1
for
every
X
gives a natural transformation from the
