We also have the following associativity and unit
laws for
\gamma
.

Theorem 3.76
.

For taut functors
F,G,H
we have the following
commutativities:

(1)

\textstyle{(\Delta{[}H\circ G{]}\circ F)\times\Delta{[}F{]}}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}H\circ G\circ F{]}\hbox to0.0pt{\ ,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{H\circ G,F}}
\textstyle{(\Delta{[}H{]}\circ G\circ F)\times(\Delta{[}G{]}\circ F)\times\Delta{[}F{]}}
\textstyle{(\Delta{[}H\circ G{]}\circ F)\times\Delta{[}F{]}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{H,G}\circ F\times\id}
\textstyle{(\Delta{[}H{]}\circ G\circ F)\times(\Delta{[}G{]}\circ F)\times\Delta{[}F{]}}
\textstyle{(\Delta{[}H{]}\circ G\circ F)\times\Delta{[}G\circ F{]}}
\scriptstyle{\id\times\gamma_{G,F}}
\textstyle{(\Delta{[}H{]}\circ G\circ F)\times\Delta{[}G\circ F{]}}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}H\circ G\circ F{]}\hbox to0.0pt{\ ,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{H,G\circ F}}

(2)

\textstyle{1\times\Delta{[}F{]}}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}F{]}\hbox to0.0pt{\ ,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(\Delta{[}\Id{]}\circ F)\times\Delta{[}F{]}}
\textstyle{1\times\Delta{[}F{]}}
\textstyle{(\Delta{[}\Id{]}\circ F)\times\Delta{[}F{]}}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}\Id\circ F{]}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{\Id,F}}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}\Id\circ F{]}}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}F{]}\hbox to0.0pt{\ ,\hss}}

(3)

\textstyle{\Delta{[}F{]}\times 1}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}F{]}\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(\Delta{[}F{]}\circ\Id)\times\Delta{[}\Id{]}}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}F{]}\times 1}
\textstyle{(\Delta{[}F{]}\circ\Id)\times\Delta{[}\Id{]}}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}F\circ\Id{]}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{F,\Id}}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}F\circ\Id{]}}
\textstyle{\Delta{[}F{]}\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}

Proof.

(1) Let
A
be a set. We’ll show that

\textstyle{HG(FA+1)\times F(A+1)}
\textstyle{HGF(A+1)}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{HG,F}(A)}
\textstyle{H(GFA+1)\times G(FA+1)\times F(A+1)}
\textstyle{HG(FA+1)\times F(A+1)}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{HG}(FA)\times\id}
\textstyle{H(GFA+1)\times G(FA+1)\times F(A+1)}
\textstyle{H(GFA+1)\times GF(A+1)}
\scriptstyle{\id\times\gamma_{G,F}(A)}
\textstyle{H(GFA+1)\times GF(A+1)}
\textstyle{HGF(A+1)}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{H,GF}(A)}

commutes. Evaluate this diagram at an element

(x,y,z)
of the domain

\textstyle{(H(\phi_{y})(z),x)}
\textstyle{HG(\phi_{x})H(\phi_{y})(z)\begin{array}[]{c}?\\
\\
=\end{array}H(\phi_{w})(z)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\textstyle{(z,y,x)}
\textstyle{(H(\phi_{y})(z),x)}
\textstyle{(z,y,x)}
\textstyle{(x,G(\phi_{x})(y))=(z,w)}
\textstyle{(x,G(\phi_{x})(y))=(z,w)}
\textstyle{HG(\phi_{x})H(\phi_{y})(z)\begin{array}[]{c}?\\
\\
=\end{array}H(\phi_{w})(z)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}

So we have to show that
HG(\phi_{x})H(\phi_{y})=H(\phi_{w})
for
w=G(\phi_{x})(y)
, and it is
sufficient to show that
G(\phi_{x})\phi_{y}=\phi_{w}
, i.e. that

\textstyle{GFA+1}
\textstyle{G(FA+1)}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{y}}
\textstyle{GFA+1}
\textstyle{GF(A+1)}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{w}}
\textstyle{G(FA+1)}
\textstyle{GF(A+1)}
\scriptstyle{G(\phi_{x})}

commutes. Restricting to the summands we
have

\textstyle{GFA}
\textstyle{G(FA+1)}
\scriptstyle{Gj_{FA}}
\textstyle{GFA}
\textstyle{GF(A+1)}
\scriptstyle{GF(j_{A})}
\textstyle{G(FA+1)}
\textstyle{GF(A+1)}
\scriptstyle{G{[}Fj_{A},x{]}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{G(FA+1)}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{GF(A+1)}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{G(FA+1)}
\textstyle{GF(A+1)}
\scriptstyle{G(\phi_{x})}

each of which commutes, the first by functoriality
of
G
, the second by definition of
w
. This
proves (1).

In (2) (and (3)) we denote the identity functor on

{\bf Set}
, which we have been calling
X
,
by
\Id
. Also
1
denotes the constant functor
with value
1
, i.e. the terminal endofunctor.
Then
\Delta[\Id](X)=(X+1)\setminus X=1
,
i.e.
\Delta[\Id]=1
. To calculate

\gamma_{\Id,F}
, take
x\in F(A+1)
and
consider
\gamma
on the
x^{th}
summand:

\Id(\phi_{x})\colon\Id(FA+1)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\Id F(A+1)

i.e. just
\phi_{x}\colon FA+1\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>F(A+1)
. Then

y\in\Delta[\Id](FA)
must be
*
and

\phi_{x}(*)=x
. So

\gamma_{\id,F}(*,x)=x

which is what (2) is asserting.

For (3) consider

\gamma\colon F(\Id A+1)\times\Id(A+1)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>F\Id(A+1)

i.e.

\gamma\colon F(A+1)\times(A+1)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>F(A+1)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

For
x\in A+1
,
\phi_{x}\colon A+1\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>A+1
is the
identity on
A
but
\phi_{x}(*)=x
. If we take

x\in\Delta[\Id](A)
,
x
must be
*
so

\phi_{*}=1_{A+1}\colon A+1\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>A+1
and

\gamma_{*}=1_{F(A+1)}
. This, when restricted to

\Delta{[}F{]}(A)\times 1
, is what (3) says.
∎

Getting a comparison

\gamma\colon\Delta{[}G{]}\circ F\times\Delta{[}F{]}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.49997pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 8.99994pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.49313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.49313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.98627pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\Delta{[}G\circ F{]}

in that direction is a bit surprising. Normally we would
expect a morphism
into
a product and, as

\Delta{[}G\circ F{]}
is a kind of cokernel, a morphism
out of it. One might think that there is a comparison
in the reverse direction for which
\gamma
is a
splitting. Looking more carefully we see that it seems
unlikely because we would need a natural transformation

\Delta{[}G\circ F{]}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\Delta{[}F{]}\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

So from an element of
GF(X+1)
we would need to
construct an element of
F(X+1)
in a natural way.
Nothing comes to mind but it’s a bit difficult to pin down precisely.

Consider the following example which illustrates well
the nature of
\gamma
. Let
F(X)
be arbitrary
(taut) and
G(X)=X^{3}
. Then
\Delta{[}G{]}(X)=3X^{2}+3X+1
so that

\Delta{[}G{]}(FX)\times\Delta{[}F{]}(X)=3\Bigl(F(X)^{2}\times\Delta{[}F{]}(X)\Bigr)+3\Bigl(F(X)\times\Delta{[}F{]}(X)\Bigr)+\Delta{[}F{]}(X)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

On the other hand
(G\circ F)(X)=F(X)\times F(X)\times F(X)
so we can use the product rule
(Theorem
3.71
) (three times) to get

\Delta{[}G\circ F{]}(X)=3\Bigl(F(X)^{2}\times\Delta{[}F{]}X\Bigr)+3\Bigl(F(X)\times(\Delta{[}F{]}(X))^{2}\Bigr)+\Bigl(\Delta{[}F{]}(X)\Bigr)^{3}\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

Then
\gamma
is the identity on the first three of
the seven summands, and given by diagonals on
the remaining ones. As
\gamma
is component-wise
on the summands, any splitting would have to be too.
For the first three summands there is only one choice, but
for summands of the form
F(X)\times\Delta{[}F{]}(X)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>F(X)\times\Delta{[}F{]}(X)^{2}
we have the two
projections, and for the last summand there are three.
This gives us
2\cdot 2\cdot 2\cdot 3=24

“canonical” splittings (for
\gamma
at
F
and

G
). But there may be many more depending
on
F
. The simplest possible
\Delta{[}F{]}(X)

is
X+1
for
F(X)=X^{[2]}=X^{2}/S_{2}
. Then
the component of
\gamma
on the last summand

\Delta{[}F{]}(X)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(\Delta{[}F{]}(X))^{3}\quad=\quad(X+1\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.49313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.49313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.98627pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>X^{3}+3X^{2}+3X+1)

\begin{array}[]{rl}x&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.49313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.49313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.98627pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(x,x,x)\\
{*}&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.49313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.49313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.98627pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>*\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}\end{array}

Now there are only three splittings of
X\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>X^{3}
,
the projections (Yoneda) and only one for
1\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>1
,
but it’s arbitrary for the six terms in the middle, giving
648 splittings, by our count (could be wrong, but
there are lots of them). But none is natural in
G
.
Any permutation of
3
,
\sigma\in S_{n}
gives
an automorphism of
G
which percolates down
to the same permutation on the
X^{3}
in

(\Delta{[}F{]}(X))^{3}
, so no one projection would be
invariant. The upshot is that there is no global
splitting of
\gamma
natural in
F
and
G
.

It will be useful in Subsection
4.5
if we express the difference
operator with its lax chain rule in tangent category
terms (see
[
6
]
for definitions). Our tangent
space for
{\bf Set}
is
P_{1}\colon{\bf Set}\times{\bf Set}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf Set}
. Given a taut functor

F\colon{\bf Set}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf Set}
, we define

D(F)\colon{\bf Set}\times{\bf Set}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf Set}\times{\bf Set}
by

D(F)(A,B)=(FA,\Delta{[}F{]}(A)\times B)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

Note that
D(F)
is “linear” in the second variable,
i.e.
D(F)(A,-)
preserves colimits, and as such is
completely determined by
D(F)(A,1)
.

The chain rule says that
D
is a monoidal functor
on taut endofunctors.

Theorem 3.77
.

Let
{\bf End}_{Taut}({\bf Set})
and

{\bf End}_{Taut}({\bf Set}\times{\bf Set})

be the strict monoidal categories of taut
endofunctors on
{\bf Set}
and
{\bf Set}\times{\bf Set}
respectively, with taut natural
transformations, and tensor given by composition.
Then
D
defines a monoidal functor

{\bf End}_{Taut}({\bf Set})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf End}_{Taut}({\bf Set}\times{\bf Set})\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

In the present context we might even go so far as to write
a general object of
{\bf Set}\times{\bf Set}
as
(A,\Delta A)

where
\Delta A
is just another object, independent
of
A
but thought of as an increment in
A
,
just like the
dx
in
f(x)dx
. Then

D(F)(A,\Delta A)=(FA,\Delta{[}F{]}(A)\times\Delta A)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

4
Differences for the special classes

We give explicit formulas for the
difference operator on the various classes of
taut functors studied in Section
2
.

4.1
Polynomials

Let
F(X)=X^{A}
for some set
A
. An element

\phi\in\Delta{[}F{]}(X)=(X+1)^{A}\setminus X^{A}

is a function
\phi\colon A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>X+1
with
*\in{\rm Im}(\phi)
(
*
is the unique element of
1
). If

S=\{a\in A|\phi(a)\neq*\}
is the support of

\phi
, then
\phi
is uniquely determined by its
restriction to
S
, and the condition
*\in{\rm Im}(\phi)
is that
S
is a proper subset of
A
. This
produces a bijection

\Delta[X^{A}]\cong\sum_{S\subsetneq A}X^{S}

where the coproduct is taken over all proper subsets
of
A
. The bijection is natural as

(f+1)^{A}\colon(X+1)^{A}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(Y+1)^{A}
preserves
support for any
f\colon X\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Y
.

A polynomial functor is a small coproduct of
powers

P(X)=\sum_{i\,\in\,I}X^{A_{i}}

for a family
\langle A_{i}\rangle_{i\,\in\,I}
of sets
and as
\Delta
commutes with coproducts we have

\Delta[P](X)=\sum_{S\subsetneq A_{i}}X^{S}

where the coproduct is taken over all
i\in I
and
proper subsets
S\subsetneq A_{i}
.

If
A
is a finite cardinal
n
, we can group like
powers of
X
together to get

\Delta[X^{n}]=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{k}X^{k}

where
\binom{n}{k}
is,
as usual, the binomial coefficient.

This is all we need for the following result.

Proposition 4.0
.

(1)

If
P(X)
is a polynomial functor
then so is
\Delta[P](X)

(2)

If
P(X)
is a power series functor, so is

\Delta[P](X)

(3)

If
P(X)
is a finitary polynomial functor,
so is
\Delta[P](X)
.

4.2
Divided powers

Let
n
be a positive integer. Then

X^{[n]}=X^{n}/S_{n}
consists of equivalence
classes of
n
-tuples
[x_{1}\dots x_{n}]
with

[x_{1}\dots x_{n}]=[y_{1}\dots y_{n}]
iff there is
a permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
such that

y_{i}=x_{\sigma(i)}
for all
i
. So

\Delta[X^{[n]}]
consists of such equivalence
classes with
x_{i}\in X
or
x_{i}=*
for all

i
and at least one
x_{i}=*
. Within each of
these equivalence classes there are those

n
-tuples with all the
*
’s at the end. If
there are
k

x
’s for
X
(and
n-k
stars)
then the equivalence relation reduces to the
existence of
\sigma\in S_{k}
with
y_{i}=x_{\sigma i}
.
This way we see that

\Delta[X^{[n]}]\cong X^{[0]}+X^{[1]}+\dots+X^{[n-1]}.

If
FX
is a divided power series

FX=C_{0}+C_{1}X^{[1]}+C_{2}X^{[2]}+\dots=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}C_{n}X^{[n]}

then

\displaystyle\Delta{[}F{]}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}C_{i}+\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}C_{i}\right)X^{[1]}+\left(\sum_{i=3}^{\infty}C_{i}\right)X^{[2]}+\dots

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}C_{i}\right)X^{[n]}.

Proposition 4.0
.

If
F(X)
is a divided power series then so is

\Delta{[}F{]}(X)
.

\Delta[X^{[A]}]
for infinite sets
A
is more
complicated. For example, for
A={\mathbb{N}}
,
we have for each
n
, equivalence classes of
the form
(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},*,*,*,\dots)

giving an
X^{[n]}
summand, but there are also
equivalence classes of the form

(***\dots*x_{n}x_{n+1}x_{n+2}\dots),n>0

giving countably many
X^{[{\mathbb{N}}]}

summands. And there’s even one more type of
equivalence class with infinitely many
*
’s
and infinitely many
x_{i}\in X
, which we can
represent as

(x_{0},*,x_{2},*,x_{4},*,\dots)

This gives us an extra copy of
X^{[{\mathbb{N}}]}
.
Thus

\Delta[X^{[{\mathbb{N}}]}]\cong{\mathbb{N}}\times X^{[{\mathbb{N}}]}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}X^{[n]}\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

A more general approach will lead to a
better understanding. Let
G
be a group and

A
a left
G
-set. Then

\Delta[X^{A}/G]

can be described as follows.
G
acts on the set

P^{\prime}A
of proper subsets of
A
by application
of the action element-wise

B\subsetneq A\quad\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.91829pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.9945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.98901pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\quad gB=\{gb\,|\,b\in B\}.

Let
S\subseteq P^{\prime}A
be a choice of
representative for each orbit of this action.
Then we have:

Proposition 4.0
.

\Delta[X^{A}/G]\cong\sum_{B\in S}A^{B}/{\rm Stab}(B)

where
{\rm Stab}(B)=\{g\in G\,|\,gB=B\}
, the
stabilizer of
B
.

Proof.

An element of
\Delta[X^{A}/G]
is an equivalence
class
[f]
of functions
f\colon A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>X+1
with

*\in{\rm Im}(f)
. The equivalence relation is given by

f\sim f^{\prime}
iff there is
g\in G
with

\textstyle{X+1}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{X+1}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{g\cdot(\ )}
.

A function into
X+1
is equivalent to a partial
function

A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.9945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.9945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.98901pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\ B\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.4945pt\raise 5.83888pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\bar{f}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.9945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.98901pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>X

and
*\in{\rm Im}(f)
is equivalent to
B

being a proper subset of
A
. The equivalence
relation translates to the existence of
g\in G

such that

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{g\cdot(\ )}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g\cdot(\ )}
Pb
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{{\bar{f}}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{{\bar{f}}}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}}

(*)

and the square being a pullback is equivalent
to
g(B)=B^{\prime}
.

Any
(B^{\prime},{\bar{f}}^{\prime})
is equivalent to a
(B,{\bar{f}})

with
B\in S
(there’s a
g\in G
which maps

B^{\prime}
to
B
, and defines
{\bar{f}}
by
(*)
above),
and for these
(B,f)
the equivalence relation
reduces to
(*)
with
g\in{\rm Stab}(B)
.
∎

Corollary 4.0
.

The class of (generalized) divided power series is
closed under the difference operator
\Delta
.

4.3
Analytic functors

Proposition 4.0
.

Let
C
be a left
S_{n}
-set (
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
). Then
there exist
S_{k}
-sets
C_{k}
,
k=0,1,\dots,n-1

such that

\Delta[X^{n}\otimes_{S_{n}}C]\cong\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}X^{k}\otimes_{S_{k}}C_{k}\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

Proof.

An element of
\Delta[X^{n}\otimes_{S_{n}}C]=\bigl((X+1)^{n}\otimes_{S_{n}}C\bigr)\setminus(X^{n}\otimes_{S_{n}}C)

is an equivalence class
[x_{1},\dots,x_{n};c]

where
c\in C
and
x_{i}\in X
or
x_{i}=*

for all
i
, with at least one
*
.
[x_{1},\dots,x_{n};c]=[y_{1},\dots,y_{n};d]
iff there exists
\sigma\in S_{n}

such that
y_{i}=x_{\sigma i}
for all
i
and

c=\sigma d
.

The number of
*
’s is invariant under the action
of
S_{n}
so is an invariant of the equivalence
class, but it is also invariant under the functorial
action (i.e. for functions
f\colon X\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Y
),
so that
\Delta[X^{n}\otimes_{S_{n}}C]

decomposes into a coproduct

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\Phi_{k}

of endofunctors
\Phi_{k}
, where
k
is the
number of
x
’s that are
not
stars.

Let
C_{k}
be the set of equivalence classes

[c]
for
c\in C
with
c\sim d
iff there is

\sigma\in S_{n}
such that
\sigma(i)=i

for all
i\leq k
and
c=\sigma d
.
S_{k}

acts on
C_{k}
by
\tau[c]=[{\bar{\tau}}c]

where
{\bar{\tau}}
is
\tau
extended to a
permutation on
n
by the identity,
i.e.

{\bar{\tau}}(i)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tau(i)&1\leq i\leq k\\
i&k<i\leq n\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}\end{array}\right.

The action is well defined because such
an extension will commute with the
\sigma
’s
above as they act on disjoint sets. So

\tau[c]=[{\bar{\tau}}c]=[{\bar{\tau}}\sigma d]=[\sigma{\bar{\tau}}d]=[{\bar{\tau}}d]\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

The claim is that
\Phi_{k}\cong X^{k}\otimes_{S_{k}}C_{k}
.
Each equivalence class in
\Phi_{k}
has a representative
of the form
[x_{1},\dots x_{k},*,\dots,*;c]
(several in fact).

We define

\phi\colon\Phi_{k}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>X^{k}\otimes_{S_{k}}C_{k}

\phi[x_{1}\dots x_{k},*,\dots,*;c]=[x_{1},\dots x_{k},[c]]\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

\phi
is well defined. Indeed, let
[x_{1},\dots,x_{k},*,\dots,*;c]=[y_{1}\dots y_{k},*,\dots,*;d]
so there
exists
\sigma\in S_{n}
such that
c=\sigma d
,

y_{i}=x_{\sigma i}
, and the
*
’s get permuted
among themselves. Let
\tau\in S_{k}
be
\sigma

restricted to
\{1,\dots,k\}
, and
{\bar{\tau}}
the
extension of
\tau
to
\{1,\dots,n\}
by the
identity on
i>k
. Then

\tau[d]=[{\bar{\tau}}d]=[{\bar{\tau}}\sigma^{-1}c]=[c]

because
{\bar{\tau}}
and
\sigma
agree on

i\leq k
, i.e.
{\bar{\tau}}\sigma^{-1}(i)=i
.
As we have
y_{i}=x_{\sigma(i)}=x_{\tau(i)}

for
i\leq k
, we have

[x_{1},\dots,x_{k};[c]]=[y_{1},\dots,y_{k};[d]]

and
\phi
is well defined.

We show that
\phi
is one to one. Suppose
[x_{1},\dots,x_{k};[c]]=[y_{1},\dots,y_{k};[d]]
, i.e. there exists
\tau\in S_{k}

such that
y_{i}=x_{\tau i}
and
\tau[d]=[c]
. Thus

[{\bar{\tau}}d]=[c]
(
{\bar{\tau}}
as above) which
means there is a
\sigma\in S_{n}
such that

\sigma(i)=i
for
i\leq k
and
c=\sigma{\bar{\tau}}d
.
For
i>k
,
\sigma(i)>k
so

\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma{\bar{\tau}}(i)=\tau(i)&i\leq k\\
\sigma{\bar{\tau}}(i)>k&i>k\end{array}

giving our permutation in
S_{n}
making

[x_{1}\dots x_{n},*,\dots,*;c]=[y_{1},\dots y_{k},*,\dots,*;d]\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

It is clear that
\phi
is onto and just as clearly natural,
and so is the required isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 4.0
.

If
C\colon{\bf Bij}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf Set}
is a species and

FX=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}X^{n}\otimes_{S_{n}}C(n)

the corresponding analytic functor, then

\Delta{[}F{]}(X)\cong\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}X^{n}\otimes_{S_{n}}\left(\sum_{l=n+1}^{\infty}C(l)_{n}\right)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

Proof.

By the proposition

\Delta{[}F{]}(X)\cong\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}X^{k}\otimes_{S_{k}}C(n)_{k}\right)

and grouping like powers of
X
together we
get the desired result.
∎

The
C_{k}
above may seem mysterious,
at the very least a bit opaque. Passing to
permutations on infinite sets will force us into
a more conceptual presentation. Rather than
choose a proper subset of each cardinality as
we did in the case of
[n]
we consider them
all at once.

Let
A
be an arbitrary set and construct a
groupoid
{\mathbb{P}}(A)
(
{\mathbb{P}}

for proper powerset) whose objects are proper
subsets
A_{0}\subsetneq A
and whose morphisms
are bijections
\sigma
of
A
preserving the
subset, i.e.

A_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>B_{0}\over{\sigma\colon A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>A\mbox{\quad s.t.\quad}\sigma A_{0}=B_{0}\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}

For each set
X
we get a functor

X^{(\ )}\colon{\mathbb{P}}(A)^{op}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf Set}

with
X^{A_{0}}
the set of functions

Let

Proposition 4.0
.

\Delta[X^{A}\otimes_{S_{A}}C]\cong\int^{A_{0}\in{\mathbb{P}}(A)}X^{A_{0}}\times{\bar{C}}.

Proof.

An element of
\Delta[X^{A}\otimes_{S_{A}}C]
is
an equivalence class of pairs
[f\colon A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>X+1,c]

for
f
a function with
*

in its image and
c\in C
, with
[f,c]=[g,d]
iff
there is
\sigma\in S_{A}
with
f=g\sigma
and

d=\sigma c
.
f\colon A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>X+1
is a partial
map

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A_{0}}
\textstyle{X}

\scriptstyle{f_{0}}

with
A_{0}
a proper subset, and
f=g\sigma

means

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B_{0}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A_{0}}

\scriptstyle{\sigma}

\scriptstyle{\sigma_{0}}

\scriptstyle{f_{0}}

\scriptstyle{g_{0}}
.

This is exactly a description of a general
element of
\int^{A_{0}}X^{A_{0}}\times C
.
∎

We can simplify things by choosing a
skeleton of
{\mathbb{P}}(A)
.
A_{0}\cong B_{0}

iff there exists
\sigma\colon A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>A
in
S_{A}

such that
\sigma A_{0}=B_{0}
which implies
that the cardinality of
A_{0}
is equal to that of

B_{0}
,
\#A_{0}=\#B_{0}
, but the complements
of
A_{0}
and
B_{0}
also have the same
cardinality,
\#A^{\prime}_{0}=\#B^{\prime}_{0}
. And this is sufficient
to have
A_{0}\cong B_{0}
. Thus for every pair
of non-zero cardinals
\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})

such that
\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}=\#A
, we choose
a subset
A_{\kappa}\subsetneq A
with

\#A_{\kappa}=\kappa_{i}
and
\#A^{\prime}_{\kappa}=\kappa_{2}
, and take the full subcategory

{\mathbb{P}}^{\prime}(A)
of
{\mathbb{P}}(A)

determined by these.
{\mathbb{P}}^{\prime}(A)
is now
the coproduct of the groups
S_{A_{\kappa}}\times S_{A^{\prime}_{\kappa}}
, which are the groups of automorphisms
of the
A_{k}
. The coend is then the coproduct of the
coends over each of these groups, which is what
we’ve been writing as the tensor product. Thus
we have the following:

Corollary 4.0
.

\Delta(X^{A}\otimes_{S_{A}}C)=\sum_{\kappa}X^{A_{\kappa}}\otimes_{S_{A_{\kappa}}\times S_{A^{\prime}_{\kappa}}}C

where the coproduct is taken over
\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})

with
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
and
\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}=\#A
.

This is still not in the form we would like. What we would
like is to express it in terms of
\otimes
over
symmetric groups. However

X^{A_{\kappa}}\otimes_{S_{A_{\kappa}}\times S_{A^{\prime}_{\kappa}}}C\cong X^{A_{\kappa}}\otimes_{S_{A_{\kappa}}}\left(S_{A_{\kappa}}\otimes_{S_{A_{\kappa}}\times S_{A^{\prime}_{\kappa}}}C\right)

where the
S_{A_{\kappa}}
in the middle is given
the left
S_{A_{\kappa}}
action by left multiplication
and the right
S_{A_{\kappa}}\times S_{A^{\prime}_{\kappa}}
action by right multiplication after
projecting onto
S_{A_{\kappa}}
.

We can further analyze
S_{A_{\kappa}}\otimes_{S_{A_{\kappa}}\times S_{A^{\prime}_{\kappa}}}C
. An element is an equivalence class
of pairs
(\sigma,c)
with
\sigma\in S_{A_{\kappa}}
and

c\in C
, with
(\sigma,c)\sim(\tau,d)
iff
there exist
\rho\in S_{A_{\kappa}}
and
\rho^{\prime}\in S_{A^{\prime}_{\kappa}}
such that

\tau=\sigma\rho\quad\mbox{and}\quad c=(\rho+\rho^{\prime})d\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

Each equivalence class has representatives
of the form
(\id,c)
and for these the equivalence
relation reduces to
(\id,c)\sim(\id,d)
iff
there exists
\rho^{\prime}\in S_{A^{\prime}_{\kappa}}
such that

c=(\id+\rho^{\prime})d
. So

S_{A_{\kappa}}\otimes_{S_{A_{\kappa}}\times S_{A^{\prime}_{\kappa}}}C\cong C_{\kappa}

with
C_{\kappa}=C/\sim
where

c\sim d\Leftrightarrow\exists\rho^{\prime}\in S_{A_{\kappa^{\prime}}}(c=(\id+\rho^{\prime})d)\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

The upshot of this long discussion is that:

Corollary 4.0
.

\Delta[X^{A}\otimes_{S_{A}}C)]=\sum X^{A_{\kappa}}\otimes_{A_{\kappa}}C_{\kappa}\hbox to0.0pt{\ ,\hss}

and
\Delta
of a generalized analytic functor is
again one.
